</div>
</details>

### 5.3.21 {#5-3-21}

Let A, P, and D be $n \times n$ matrices. Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. A is diagonalizable if $A = PDP^{-1}$ for some matrix D and some invertible matrix P

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**D.** The statement is false. The symbol D does not automatically denote a diagonal matrix.

</div>
</details>

### 5.3.22 {#5-3-22}

Let A be an $n \times n$ matrix. Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. If $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ has a basis of eigenvectors of A, then A is diagonalizable

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**D.** The statement is true. A is diagonalizable if and only if there are enough eigenvectors to form a basis of $\mathbb{R}^{n}$.

</div>
</details>

### 5.3.26 {#5-3-26}

Let A be an $n \times n$ matrix. Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. If A is diagonalizable, then A has n distinct eigenvalues

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**B.** The statement is false. A diagonalizable matrix can have fewer than n eigenvalues and still have n linearly independent eigenvectors.

</div>
</details>

### 5.3.27 {#5-3-27}

Assume A, P, and D are $n \times n$ matrices. Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. If AP = PD, with D diagonal, then the nonzero columns of P must be eigenvectors of A

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**A.** Choose the correct answer below. Your answer is correct

</div>
</details>

### 5.3.30 {#5-3-30}

A is $a_{3} \times 3$ matrix with two eigenvalues. Each eigenspace is one-dimensional. Is A diagonalizable? Why?

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**B.** No. The sum of the dimensions of the eigenspaces $= 2 2$ and the matrix has 3 columns. The sum of the dimensions of the eigenspace and the number of columns must be equal.

</div>
</details>

### 5.3.1 {#5-3-1}

Let $A = PDP^{-1}$ and P and D as shown below. Compute $A^{4}$. $P = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 2 & 7 \end{bmatrix}$, $D = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

$A^{4} = \begin{bmatrix} 641 & -320 \\ 1120 & -559 \end{bmatrix}$(Simplify your answer.)

</div>
</details>

### 5.3.2 {#5-3-2}

Let $A = PDP^{-1}$ and P and D as shown below. Compute $A^{4}$. $P = \begin{bmatrix} 2 & -3 \\ -3 & 5 \end{bmatrix}$, $D = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & one half \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

$A^{4} = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{151}{16} & \frac{45}{8} \\ -\frac{225}{16} & -\frac{67}{8} \end{bmatrix}$(Simplify your answer.)

</div>
</details>

### 5.3.3 {#5-3-3}

Use the factorization $A = PDP^{-1}$ to compute $A^{k}$, where k represents an arbitrary integer.$\begin{bmatrix} a & 5 (b - a) \\ 0 & b \end{bmatrix}$=$\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 5 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} a & 0 \\ 0 & b \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -5 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

$A^{k} = \begin{bmatrix} a^{k} & 5 (b^{k} - a^{k}) \\ 0 & b^{k} \end{bmatrix}$

</div>
</details>

## §6.1 Inner Product, Length, and Orthogonality {#6-1-inner-product-length-and-orthogonality}

### 6.1.1 {#6-1-1}

Compute$\mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{u}$,$\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{u}$, and $\frac{ \mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{u}$}{$\mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{u}$} using the vectors$\mathbf{u}$=$\begin{bmatrix} -3 \\ 5 \end{bmatrix}$and$\mathbf{v}$=$\begin{bmatrix} 7 \\ 4 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

$\mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{u} = 34$ (Simplify your answer.) Part 2$\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{u} = -1$ (Simplify your answer.) Part $3 \frac{ \mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{u}$}{$\mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{u}$} = negative $\frac{1}{34}$ (Type an integer or a simplified fraction.)

</div>
</details>

### 6.1.2 {#6-1-2}

Compute$\mathbf{w} \times \mathbf{w}$,$\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{w}$, and $\frac{ \mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{w}$}{$\mathbf{w} \times \mathbf{w}$} using the vectors$\mathbf{w}$=$\begin{bmatrix} 3 \\ -4 \\ -5 \end{bmatrix}$and$\mathbf{x}$=$\begin{bmatrix} 5 \\ -3 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

$\mathbf{w} \times \mathbf{w} = 50$ (Simplify your answer. Type an integer or a simplified fraction.) Part 2$\mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{w} = 22$ (Simplify your answer. Type an integer or a simplified fraction.) Part $3 \frac{ \mathbf{x} \times \mathbf{w}$}{$\mathbf{w} \times \mathbf{w}$} $= \frac{11}{25}$ (Simplify your answer. Type an integer or a simplified fraction.)

</div>
</details>

### 6.1.5 {#6-1-5}

Compute the quantity using the vectors$\mathbf{u}$=$\begin{bmatrix} -5 \\ 2 \end{bmatrix}$, and$\mathbf{v}$=$\begin{bmatrix} 4 \\ 5 \end{bmatrix}$. $(\frac{ \mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{v}$}{$\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{v}$})$\mathbf{v}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

$(\frac{ \mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{v}$}{$\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{v}$})$\mathbf{v}$=$\begin{bmatrix} \square$negative $\frac{40}{41}$ negative $\frac{50}{41}$ (Simplify your answers.)

</div>
</details>

### 6.1.7 {#6-1-7}

Compute $\lVert \mathbf{w} \rVert$ using$\mathbf{w}$=$\begin{bmatrix} 5 \\ 3 \\ -2 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

$\lVert \mathbf{w} \rVert = \sqrt{38}$ (Type an exact answer, using radicals as needed.)

</div>
</details>

Find a unit vector in the direction of the given vector.$\begin{bmatrix} 27 \\ -36 \end{bmatrix}$

A unit vector in the direction of the given vector is$\begin{bmatrix} three fifths \\ -four fifths \end{bmatrix}$. (Type an exact answer, using radicals as needed.)

Find a unit vector in the direction of the given vector.$\begin{bmatrix} -3 \\ 27 \\ -3 \end{bmatrix}$

</details>

The distance between u and z is $\sqrt{66}$. (Type an exact answer, using radicals as needed.)

</details>

### 6.1.15 {#6-1-15}

Determine if the following vectors are orthogonal. $a = \begin{bmatrix} 8 \\ -7 \end{bmatrix}$, $b = \begin{bmatrix} -2 \\ -5 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>

**D.** The vectors a and b are not orthogonal because $a \times$ •b $= 19$.

</div>
</details>

### 6.1.16 {#6-1-16}

Determine if the following vectors are orthogonal. $u = \begin{bmatrix} 12 \\ 5 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}$, $v = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -5 \\ 13 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">

<div class="callout-content">

**B.** The vectors u and v are orthogonal because $u \times$ •v $= 0$.

</div>
</details>

<div class="callout-content">

**B.** The vectors u and v are orthogonal because $u \times$ •v $= 0$.

</div>
</details>

### 6.1.18 {#6-1-18}

Determine if the following vectors are orthogonal. $u = \begin{bmatrix} -2 \\ 7 \\ 5 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$, $v = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -6 \\ 19 \\ -5 \end{bmatrix}$

<div class="callout-content">

**B.** The vectors u and v are not orthogonal because $u \times$ •v $= 51$.

</div>
</details>

### 6.1.19 {#6-1-19}

Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. The vector is in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$.$\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{v} = \lVert \mathbf{v} \rVert$ squared

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**A.** The statement is true. By the definition of the length of a vector$\mathbf{v} v, \lVert v \rVert v = \sqrt{ \mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{v}$} v•v.

</div>
</details>

## §6.2 Orthogonal Sets {#6-2-orthogonal-sets}

### 6.2.2 {#6-2-2}

Determine whether the set of vectors is orthogonal.$\begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -7 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}$,$\begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 7 \end{bmatrix}$,$\begin{bmatrix} -50 \\ -7 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**A.** The set of vectors is orthogonal because each pair of distinct vectors from the set is orthogonal.

</div>
</details>

### 6.2.3 {#6-2-3}

<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

Is the set of vectors orthogonal? Your answer is correct

</div>
</details>

### 6.2.5 {#6-2-5}

Determine whether the set of vectors is orthogonal.$\begin{bmatrix} 6 \\ -3 \\ 1 \\ 6 \end{bmatrix}$,$\begin{bmatrix} -1 \\ 6 \\ -6 \\ 5 \end{bmatrix}$,$\begin{bmatrix} 12 \\ 35 \\ 33 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

Is the set of vectors orthogonal? Your answer is correct

</div>

## §6.3 Orthogonal Projections {#6-3-orthogonal-projections}

### 6.3.4 {#6-3-4}

Verify that $\{ \mathbf{u}_{1}$,$\mathbf{u}_{2} \}$ is an orthogonal set, and then find the orthogonal projection of y onto Span $\{ \mathbf{u}_{1}$,$\mathbf{u}_{2} \}$. $y = \begin{bmatrix} 6 \\ 2 \\ -5 \end{bmatrix}$,$\mathbf{u}_{1}$=$\begin{bmatrix} 5 \\ 6 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$,$\mathbf{u}_{2}$=$\begin{bmatrix} -6 \\ 5 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

To verify that $\{ \mathbf{u}_{1}$,$\mathbf{u}_{2} \}$ is an orthogonal set, find$\mathbf{u}_{1} \times \mathbf{u}_{2}$.$\mathbf{u}_{1} \times \mathbf{u}_{2} = 0$ (Simplify your answer.) Part 2 The projection of y onto Span $\{ \mathbf{u}_{1}$,$\mathbf{u}_{2} \}$ is$\begin{bmatrix} \square \\ \square \\ \square \end{bmatrix}$. (Simplify your answers.)

</div>
</details>

### 6.3.21 {#6-3-21}

Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. Assume all vectors and subspaces are in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$. If$\mathbf{z}$is orthogonal to$\mathbf{u}_{1}$and$\mathbf{u}_{2}$and if $W =$ Span $\{ \mathbf{u}_{1}$,$\mathbf{u}_{2} \}$, then$\mathbf{z}$must be in $W^{orthogonal}$

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**A.** The statement is true. Since$\mathbf{z}$ is orthogonal to$\mathbf{u}_{1}$ and$\mathbf{u}_{2}$, it is orthogonal to every vector in Span $\{ \mathbf{u}_{1}$,$\mathbf{u}_{2} \}$ Spanu1, u2, a set that spans W.

</div>
</details>

### 6.3.22 {#6-3-22}

Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. Assume all vectors and subspaces are in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$. For each$\mathbf{y}$and each subspace W, the vector$\mathbf{y}$- pro $j_{Upper}$ W$\mathbf{y}$is orthogonal to W

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

**B.** The statement is true. Because$\mathbf{y}$ can be written uniquely in the form$\mathbf{y} y =$ pro $j_{Upper}$ W$\mathbf{y}$projWy $+ + \mathbf{z}$, where pro $j_{Upper}$ W$\mathbf{y}$projWy is in W and$\mathbf{z}$ is in $W^{orthogonal}$ W⊥, it follows that$\mathbf{z} z = \mathbf{y} y -$ - pro $j_{Upper}$ W$\mathbf{y}$projWy.

</div>
</details>

### 6.3.23 {#6-3-23}

Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. Assume all vectors and subspaces are in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$. The orthogonal projection ModifyingAbove$\mathbf{y}$with caret of$\mathbf{y}$onto a subspace W can sometimes depend on the orthogonal basis for W used to compute ModifyingAbove$\mathbf{y}$with caret

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>

### 6.3.26 {#6-3-26}

Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. Assume all vectors and subspaces are in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$. If W is a subspace of $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ and if$\mathbf{v}$is in both W and $W^{orthogonal}$, then$\mathbf{v}$must be the zero vector

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

The statement is true. If$\mathbf{v}$is in W, then pro $j_{Upper}$ W$\mathbf{v}$=$\mathbf{v}$. Since the $W^{orthogonal}$ component of$\mathbf{v}$is equal to$\mathbf{v} - proj_{Upper}$ W$\mathbf{v}$, the $W^{orthogonal}$ component of$\mathbf{v}$must be$\mathbf{0}$. A similar argument can be formed for the W component of$\mathbf{v}$based on the orthogonal projection of$\mathbf{v}$onto the subspace $W^{orthogonal}$. Thus,$\mathbf{v}$must be$\mathbf{0}$

</div>
</details>

### 6.3.28 {#6-3-28}

Determine whether the statement below is true or false. Justify the answer. Assume all vectors and subspaces are in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$. If$\mathbf{y}$=$\mathbf{z}_{1}$+$\mathbf{z}_{2}$, where$\mathbf{z}_{1}$is in a subspace W and$\mathbf{z}_{2}$is in $W^{orthogonal}$, then$\mathbf{z}_{1}$must be the orthogonal projection of$\mathbf{y}$onto W

<details class="callout" data-callout="success" data-callout-fold="-">
<summary class="callout-title"><div class="callout-icon"><svg class="lucide lucide-check" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><use href="#cicon-check"/></svg></div><div class="callout-title-inner">Answer</div><div class="callout-fold"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-chevron-down"><path d="m6 9 6 6 6-6"/></svg></div></summary>
<div class="callout-content">

The statement is true. Since the orthogonal decomposition of$\mathbf{y}$into components that exist in W and $W^{orthogonal}$ is unique,$\mathbf{z}_{1}$must correspond to the orthogonal projection of$\mathbf{y}$onto W

</div>
</details>
